TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* What Does China’s New Military Base in Laos Mean?
  Foreign Policy | 07 Oct 05:00 UTC
  Beijing’s third overseas base is also its second in Southeast Asia.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: China opens a new military base in Laos ; a rumor that Trump will attend the next ASEAN summit spreads; and the hairy rhinos of Sumatra are expecting babies. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Southeast Asia Brief. The highlights this week: China opens a new military base in Laos ; a rumor that Trump will attend the next ASEAN summit spreads; and the hairy rhinos of Sumatra are expecting babies. What Does China’s New Military Base in Laos Mean? China has established a military base in Laos. It is its third overseas military base and its second in Southeast Asia; the other two are in Cambodia and Djibouti. The China-Laos Joint Support and Training Center and the China-Laos Friendship Aviation Academy are situated at an airport about 30 miles from Laos’s capital, Vientiane, according to an announcement last week from Beijing’s Defense Ministry. Satellite imagery shows that work started last year, according to Reuters . This will be China’s first overseas air force base. The ministry said that the facilities exist to help Laos train its air force pilots. Data on the Lao People’s Liberation Army Air Force is sketchy, but open-source intelligence firm Janes reckons that it operates five SAIC LE500 basic trainers and four Yakovlev Yak-130UTS advanced jet trainers. China’s global military presence is dwarfed by that of the United States. But in 2017, it opened its first acknowledged overseas military base, a logistics base in Djibouti, where it has a permanent presence. In 2025, it opened a joint training and support center at Ream Naval Base in Cambodia, located on the Gulf of Thailand. However, Cambodia has insisted that there are no permanently stationed Chinese personnel or warships based there. And vessels from other navies, including the U.S. Navy, have visited Ream. If China now develops a meaningful presence in Laos, what does this change? China-focused military commentator Rudy Lu suggested that the base would allow China to shape Laos’s air force and encourage Laos to purchase Chinese equipment. The base could also be used in annual joint aviation drills with Thailand. The commentator quoted a Chinese military analysis account that said this would make the training “closer to actual combat.” Last and not least, the account said the base could let China “exert influence in the Indochina Peninsula in the future.” Vietnam will likely be most concerned, as it has long viewed Indochina as
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/07/china-military-base-laos-vientiane/

Meanwhile, the war has exposed fundamental vulnerabilities in the United States’ Middle East posture and munitions inventories , and U.S. forces have exited the conflict exhausted and depleted. Whatever happens in the coming months, the prospects for a lasting peace between the United States and Iran are dim. Iran’s nuclear program , missile and drone arsenal , and proxy groups still stand; control of the Strait of Hormuz remains contested ; and perhaps most important, Russian and Chinese support is facilitating a rapid Iranian reconstitution . Meanwhile, the war has exposed fundamental vulnerabilities in the United States’ Middle East posture and munitions inventories , and U.S. forces have exited the conflict exhausted and depleted. The White House and Department of Defense should expect a more difficult Iran in the months ahead. In seven months, Iran has validated the utility of its drone and missile arsenal, successfully driven a wedge between Israel and its Arab neighbors, and degraded U.S. forces. Simultaneously, Iran’s mines, small boats, drones, and missiles have largely closed the Strait of Hormuz, holding global energy markets hostage . Rather than isolate Iran from Russia and China, the conflict appears to have only increased their defense industrial cooperation . Buoyed by these successes and motivated to deter future U.S. and Israeli interventions, Iran has every incentive to reconstitute its missile and drone arsenal, learn from this war, and continue its bid for regional dominance. The question for the United States is therefore not just how to end the current conflict, but how to avoid a new disaster that dwarfs the losses and damage of recent months. We recently ran two war games exploring what a future round of fighting might look like. The results were grim. An honest appraisal suggests that Washington must change its approach to missile and drone defense in the region or risk major losses. * Why Chinese Parents Don’t Want Two Kids
  Foreign Policy | 06 Oct 20:05 UTC
  Beijing is pushing for bigger families, but the realities of childbirth and early parenting make it a hard sell.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. It’s Golden Week, China’s fall national holiday period, so as the Chinese public heads to Southeast Asia for vacation or simply enjoys a break from work, we’re doing one of our periodic deep dives. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. It’s Golden Week, China’s fall national holiday period, so as the Chinese public heads to Southeast Asia for vacation or simply enjoys a break from work, we’re doing one of our periodic deep dives. This week, we’re talking about childbirth in China—and how social stress and family pressures contribute to one of the world’s lowest birth rates . Fertility almost always declines as countries get richer, but China has seen one of the sharpest drops of any nation, especially since 2017. It’s a credit to public stubbornness that during many of the one-child policy years, from 1979 to 2015, the average Chinese couple had close to two kids anyway. Now that the government wants them to have two, the average couple has only one. It was a shock to officials that loosening the policy to two children in 2015 and then to three in 2021 produced no uptick in births. Commentary tends to focus on cost: China is the second-most expensive place in the world to raise a child. The biggest expenses come later, when education costs soar. The government tried to solve this problem by banning private K-12 tutoring in 2021, but that only created a gray market where parents pay even more for tutors. Yet in talking to Chinese parents, I’ve found that pregnancy, childbirth, and postpartum care are major reasons why having a kid is a one-and-done choice for most. First, there’s the career cost for women. On paper, China has generous, universal maternity leave. (Paternity leave is much shorter and exists only as a patchwork of local rules.) But because of weak enforcement and widespread informal employment, many women do not or cannot take it —particularly those without a college degree. Even urban, professional women face a real risk of being replaced or sidelined in their careers after having children. Gender discrimination is illegal in China, but firms regularly advertise men-only roles or say that they will hire only married women who already have children. Government messaging on the topic, which has sometimes stressed that child-raising is women’s natural role, has only made matters worse . This gap between policy and reality follows women into the delivery room. The price of childbirth in Chinese public
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/06/china-birth-rate-pregnancy-fertility-children-parenting-costs/

Adam Tooze: The term that seems to be commonly used by analysts is “freezing up” or “frozen.” The mortgage rate is over 7 percent, which is lower than the 7.7 percent rate in 2023 when inflation spiked. But the crucial point is that as recently as 2022, you could get a mortgage for as little as 3 percent; during COVID, it fell even lower than that. An increase in interest rates from 3 percent to over 7 percent is a huge financing shock to what is the largest single asset class in the world. By comparison, U.S. Treasurys is a $40 trillion asset pool; American domestic real estate, so housing, is $55 trillion, of which owner-occupied is $48.7 trillion. * China Doesn’t Need to Invade Taiwan to Control It
  Foreign Policy | 06 Oct 19:19 UTC
  A bloodless political victory may be far harder for Washington to handle.
  FULL TEXT: There is fierce debate about whether the United States should , would , and could defend Taiwan in the event of a military assault by China. But there is another way events could play out that doesn’t involve a dramatic blockade or amphibious invasion: nonforcible realignment or, in a worst-case scenario, bloodless annexation, whereby an elected government in Taipei concludes a treaty effectively ceding Taiwan’s territory to the People’s Republic of China. Such a strategy, known by euphemisms like “winning without fighting” or “peaceful reunification,” is Beijing’s preferred outcome . And Washington is unprepared for it. The threat of nonforcible realignment or annexation presents distinct obstacles from an openly violent military conflict. Reversing a covert and coerced, yet seemingly voluntary, political decision in Taipei to capitulate would also be exceedingly difficult, putting a premium on prevention. With Taiwan’s next presidential election approaching in a little over a year, and the leading candidates still uncertain, the time is ripe to address this vulnerability. * Trump’s Red-Dye Diesel Plan Has a Major Flaw
  Foreign Policy | 06 Oct 16:11 UTC
  Cutting fuel taxes for truckers might bring a little relief, but it won’t add any new supplies.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The Trump administration, growing increasingly fretful of high fuel prices just a month before the U.S. midterm elections, issued late Monday a novel executive order meant to make diesel fuel a little cheaper for beleaguered truckers and farmers. It almost certainly won’t help. U.S. President Donald Trump authorized the deferral, through the end of the year, of federal taxes on diesel fuel used to power vehicles on roads and highways. (That tax revenue, like the gasoline tax, helps pay for highway maintenance.) His solution is to temporarily legalize the use of so-called red-dye diesel—a tax-free diesel flavor normally earmarked for off-road uses such as agriculture, mining, and logging—for use by truckers. The Trump administration, growing increasingly fretful of high fuel prices just a month before the U.S. midterm elections, issued late Monday a novel executive order meant to make diesel fuel a little cheaper for beleaguered truckers and farmers. It almost certainly won’t help. U.S. President Donald Trump authorized the deferral, through the end of the year, of federal taxes on diesel fuel used to power vehicles on roads and highways. (That tax revenue, like the gasoline tax, helps pay for highway maintenance.) His solution is to temporarily legalize the use of so-called red-dye diesel—a tax-free diesel flavor normally earmarked for off-road uses such as agriculture, mining, and logging—for use by truckers. There is no difference between red-dye diesel and regular diesel except the addition of red dye, which is put into the fuel to make it easier for inspectors to see if potential violators are using tax-exempt fuel where they shouldn’t; the fines and penalties are steep. Trump’s measure would, in theory, shave 24 cents off the price of a gallon of diesel, which last week was at record levels and remains very high, at $6.31 a gallon. If states follow suit with their own tax breaks, diesel could potentially be back under $6 a gallon. But there are a lot of problems with the hail-mary measure. For starters, it is only a deferral of those federal fuel taxes, not an outright suspension. Unless Congress passes legislation, at some point those deferred taxes will come due, which simply means storing up bigger bills and bigger accounting headaches for truckers down the road. “It’s one thing to waive a tax and another thing to simply defer it. This just looks like a mess, but then again, it’s all a circus,” said Tom Kl
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/06/red-dye-diesel-trump-hormuz-prices-iran-war/

So, decades later, in 2023, when photos of smoke from yet another Canadian wildfire began to appear on social media, I was surprised by how well digital photography had captured what had proved so elusive in the late disposable-camera era.   FULL TEXT: Wars do not alter the balance of power only when cities fall; it also shifts when geography acquires new value. The Houthi recapture of the areas alongside the Bab al-Mandab Strait sent shock waves through global markets and prompted a range of reactions and concerns. Although the operation’s declared strategic objective is to recapture Sanaa, its opening phase began primarily on Yemen’s western coast. The counteroffensive is, in essence, an attempt to retake territory whose continued control by the Houthis would pose a serious strategic headache for global trade and maritime security. At the same time, the latest developments could provide an opportunity for international actors to redefine Yemen’s protracted conflict and to reshape the internal military and political dynamics that have sustained it for years. For 12 years, the prevailing understanding was that Yemen’s conflict was about central control: Whoever held Sanaa held the levers of the state, commanded the army and could shape the country’s future. The Houthis were one actor in that contest: an armed group that seized Sanaa and parts of the country’s north by force, cultivated deep ties with Iran and acquired the strength and capability to threaten its adversaries inside and outside Yemen. But their recent advance and seizure of the western coast and the areas surrounding Bab al-Mandab have upended that equation. The question is no longer who controls Sanaa, or which faction holds this or that city. It is who can project power over one of the world’s most important maritime choke points, and through it affect international trade, Red Sea security and the calculations of regional and global powers.   FULL TEXT: Sample analysis tubes at the Institute of Cancer Research in Sutton, UK. Photo by Stefan Wermuth/Reuters When reading the news, you may occasionally be bemused by the flip-flopping science and health stories: one week you’re told coffee is good for you, the next week that it’s bad. Red wine extends your life, then it doesn’t. Over time, new scientific studies seemingly contradict one another, leaving us unsure what evidence to believe. As a science journalist , I’ve learnt that often the actual problem is that each study is reported in isolation, rather than weighed against everything else we know. And sometimes, the risks of harm are high. For example, in September 2025, I reported on the US Food and Drug Administration’s announcement that it would add a warning label to the painkiller acetaminophen (paracetamol), claiming that taking the drug during pregnancy increased a child’s risk of autism. It made global headlines. Yet looking closer at the science, it was clear that the FDA and its parent health department had cherrypicked a few studies, and downplayed other more robust findings. This was confirmed a couple of months later when researchers published a major review that better represented the full body of knowledge. ‘Existing evidence does not clearly link maternal paracetamol use during pregnancy with autism or ADHD in offspring,’ it concluded. By then, of course, countless women had been needlessly scared about a painkiller that is widely considered one of the safest to take during pregnancy. In 2025, academics worldwide published about 7 million scholarly articles ­– that’s more than 19,000 each day. On the surface, that might seem like a number to celebrate, but it also poses a problem. As the volume of research balloons, it can be hard to discern what these millions of papers actually tell us. Within the firehose, there are rigorous methodologies and important findings, but also baffling contradictions, unconfirmed results and sloppy science. And some diverse forms of knowledge, such as lived experience and Indigenous insights, are rarely captured in scholarly articles and databases at all. The causes are systemic. Scientists publish and promote one paper after another because that’s how they advance in their careers. Journalists breathlessly chase the latest, flashiest studies so their headlines get clicks online. Meanwhile, hardly any of us spend time trying to make sense of what the world already knows by carefully synthesising and taking st
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-life-saving-scientific-knowledge-hiding-in-plain-sight

Competitors attempt to leap from both feet to strike a suspended target, landing on the same foot used to kick, with the target rising after each successful round. The game was once used by hunters to indicate a successful hunt and demands extraordinary strength, balance and control. In her portrait, McIntyre captures the Nunamiut athlete Sean Uquqtuq in action with monochrome, hand-processed film that abstracts the landscape around him, focusing on his face and the movement of his body. Combined with an ambient score, these elements lend the piece an immersive, dreamlike quality. * The AI boom is making the world’s cheapest smartphones disappear
  Rest of World | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  AI data centers are driving demand for memory chips, pushing phone makers to raise prices and abandon some of their most affordable models.
  FULL TEXT: The world’s cheapest smartphones are disappearing because AI infrastructure is absorbing the components. Prices of existing smartphone models have risen by about 15% globally this year , while newly launched models are roughly 25% more expensive than last year. A global memory chip shortage, driven by demand from AI data centers, is pushing up the cost of making smartphones. Chinese smartphone makers — which have championed affordable, feature-rich devices and account for approximately 60% of all smartphones shipped globally — have “drastically reduced” their entry-level projects this year as memory-chip costs have risen, Ivan Lam, senior analyst at research firm Counterpoint, told Rest of World . They are instead prioritizing more profitable premium devices. The smartphone price increases vary sharply by region: They have risen by 21% in India, 19% across Asia-Pacific, and 18% in the Middle East and Africa. In the U.S., they have gone up by just 5%. The shift could affect more than consumers looking for cheaper phones. For people who have yet to buy their first smartphone, higher prices could make it harder to get online at all — potentially widening the digital divide. The consequences will also reach the U.S. as more essential services move online and the global supply chain for smartphones becomes more expensive. GSMA, the global mobile industry association, had earlier said nearly 800 million more people will use mobile internet by 2030. But it is concerned that rising smartphone costs would affect this projection and widen the gap of digital inequality, Claire Sibthorpe, head of digital inclusion at the group’s nonprofit foundation, told Rest of World . “We live in an increasingly digital world, and the proliferation of technologies such as AI is creating greater digital divides and inequities,” Sibthorpe said. “Stakeholders are increasingly using AI to provide access to important services, but people won’t be able to benefit without access to an internet-enabled phone and the internet.” In 2025, more than one in four smartphones shipped globally cost less than $150 . That market is now shrinking. Chinese phonemakers including Xiaomi i Xiaomi Xiaomi is a Chinese consumer electronics company that leads the country in smartphone manufacturing and sales. READ MORE , Oppo, Vivo, and Huawei i Huawei Huawei is a Chinese technology company focused on mobile phones and telecommunications, and is seen as a poster child for China’s global tech ambitions. * A life in episodes
  Aeon | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  For a decade, I’ve posted episodes of my memoir to Facebook. My readers correct my understanding of my past - by Thomas Söderqvist Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: When I was about 15, alone in the flat one afternoon, I sneaked into my mother’s writing desk and found a small pocket calendar from 1946 pushed to the back of a drawer. I had grown up with my mother and her parents, not knowing who my biological father was, or how I had come about; the question was simply never asked. I turned, with a kind of dread, to the entries from nine months before I was born. In mid-March there was an ordinary note – ‘Party at the Academic Association’; she was training as a laboratory technician in Lund and liked the students’ company – and on the following day, in her hand, five words: ‘God, what have I done!’ I still remember the shock of standing there with the diary, half-guilty for trespassing on her private papers, and saying aloud, over and over: ‘That’s me – that’s me. What have you done? I’m a mistake.’ For half a century, the scene stayed as it was: fixed, private, finished. Then, about a decade ago, I posted it – to several hundred old acquaintances and strangers – on Facebook. I was in doubt; I felt faint embarrassment at making it public, but this was itself part of what I wanted to examine. Why was it shameful? What did the shame tell me about myself? And would saying it out loud, to strangers, do anything to drain the memory of its sting? The answers came from my readers, and one of them showed me something I could not have seen on my own. Betty, a friend from the years we were postdocs together at Stanford, had heard me tell the story in person long ago, and she said the written version was missing something: my voice, the intonation and the look on my face. What look, I asked – had I played it for sympathy, had I ironised it? ‘Absolutely flat,’ she wrote. ‘No affect. A Max von Sydow deadpan. But yes, you ironised.’ I had not known that about myself: that I’d delivered the central wound of my origins with no expression at all and turned it into a dry joke. T hat discovery, prompted by a reader’s response, was one of the first signs that writing one’s life in public could change how memory and narrative take shape. Writing an autobiography or memoir was traditionally a solitary undertaking. Authors worked through papers, memories and past events largely on their own, occasionally consulting family, friends or colleagues. Only after years of private work would the manuscript reach the public. Readers encountered the finished account, with little sense of the revisions, doubts and shifts in understanding that had shap
  https://aeon.co/essays/what-i-have-learned-from-posting-episodes-of-my-life-on-facebook

* Sliman Mansour’s Art Captured the Palestinian Condition
  New Lines | 02 Oct 10:14 UTC
  Sliman Mansour spent a lifetime using his art to preserve Palestinian identity and connect people to their land, inspiring generations of artists in his wake. A writer who knew him from childhood as a close friend of her father remembers the gentle, humble man behind the iconic paintings. The post Sliman Mansour’s Art Captured the Palesti...
  FULL TEXT: When we speak of the Palestinian artist Sliman Mansour, the conversation too often narrows to a single painting, his “Camel of Hardships” (1973). It was through this painting that Mansour became known in Palestine and the Arab world. “Camel of Hardships” was inspired by the porters he saw on the streets of Jerusalem, when he passed through the Old City. It depicts an old man with traditional Palestinian clothes carrying the city of Jerusalem, as a heavy load, on his shoulder. The painting’s influence exploded with the decision to reproduce it as a poster, distributing and selling it at affordable prices for people to purchase and display in their homes. Seemingly every Palestinian home displayed “Camel of Hardships” on its walls. But while many were familiar with the poster, far fewer knew the artist who created it. That iconic painting has also dominated discussions since Mansour’s death on Aug. 24, at the age of 79, overshadowing the breadth of a career spanning more than five decades. Perhaps this is what made Mansour dislike that particular painting for such a long time. For him, although it was iconic and marked a phase in which he became more aware of his identity as a Palestinian artist, it nevertheless overshadowed much of his other work, work that people did not know much about until recent years, when he became active on social media. I knew Mansour as my father’s lifelong friend from my childhood onward. He was a gentle, humble man who frequented our home as if he were a member of the family. He was courteous, with a blond beard, a commanding presence and a cheerful soul. I remember him as a regular attendee of the League of Palestinian Artists meetings held on the long glass balcony at our home on Nablus Street in al-Bireh. I remember him sitting with my father for hours in our garden beneath the walnut tree on summer evenings, discussing art, exhibitions, artists’ meetings and elections. As a child, I would overhear the heated debates that punctuated these gatherings, along with the laughter and conversations fueled by boundless passion. The meetings often left behind clouds of white smoke tinged with the sweet aroma of pipe tobacco some artists favored, and ashtrays filled with ash and cigarette butts. Mansour and Nabil Anani met in the early 1970s, a few years after the 1967 war and occupation of all of historic Palestine. * Who gets to decide if AI is safe? Africa wants a say
  Rest of World | 02 Oct 10:00 UTC
  African companies race to adopt American and Chinese AI, with little attention paid to safety.
  FULL TEXT: African leaders called last week for a greater role in setting global standards for artificial intelligence. Their statements at the United Nations came amid warnings from Silicon Valley executives about AI models being misused , and sometimes going rogue and taking unauthorized actions. OpenAI’s agents hacked government websites across the world in June. Models from Anthropic , Google , and Meta , too, hacked into other companies’ systems during tests. “Africa … must be equal co-architects in determining the standards, ethics, and architectures of this technological era,” Lewis Garseedah Brown II, permanent representative of Liberia, said at a U.N. Security Council meeting on AI. African companies and governments are racing to deploy AI systems built in the U.S. and China. But many nations lack the ability to independently test whether those systems are safe for their populations, experts told Rest of World. “ There’s essentially no real discussion about AI safety. So, there’s a real vacuum,” said Jonathan Shock, associate professor in math at the University of Cape Town. If countries set up their own safeguards, it could complicate operations for American and Chinese companies in new markets. Fragmented regulations could make global deployments of models more costly and difficult. Incidents of AI misuse and malfunction are surfacing across Africa. Kenya’s predictive machine learning algorithm for setting health insurance contributions has driven up costs for poor households. Scammers in West Africa have used AI-generated content to blackmail victims in extortion schemes . The militant group Boko Haram has also used AI to organize attacks and design weapons after bypassing safeguards. “I think it’s going to take a potentially catastrophic incident for governments to really take [heed],” Shock said, which he dubbed his optimistic view. “The pessimistic view is we don’t stop it in time.” Africa currently lags behind other regions in AI adoption, though its companies are moving quickly. Out of 85 large companies surveyed by PricewaterhouseCoopers this year, more than 80% were running AI pilots. Many lack formal structures to oversee safety, according to the report. “Everyone is just trying to catch up [on AI deployment],” Mélanie Keïta, CEO and founder of Nairobi-based financing company Melanin Kapital, told Rest of World . Like many African companies, Melanin Kapital has weighed the choice between American and Chinese AI models, testing both internally. Bot

That’s how I let off my rage.’ To King, retaliation felt less like a choice than an inevitability. ‘Whether I do it or not, somebody going to do it,’ he said. ‘If it ain’t me, I got other homies who feel the same way I feel. I got cousins, family members, they mommas. That’s how it be, and that’s how it’s going to always be. It’s a repeated cycle.’ His clip was full. The Confederation of Turkish Trade Unions, the largest trade union confederation in Turkey, estimated that a single worker needed 39,123 lira, or about $916 a month, to cover basic living costs. An hour of overtime at the legal rate adds only about $4. Nature said that there is little left after basic expenses. Even buying new clothes or going to the cinema can take months of planning. Any signs you might see are low to the ground and handmade, protesting a data center, for instance, or thanking Jesus. I’d gone to this rural pocket of the country for the 90th annual Old Fiddler’s Convention in Galax, an independent city of just under 7,000 near the North Carolina border whose population, for six days in early August, balloons to around four times that. They arrive in RVs and Casitas, camper vans and cars to stake out 20-by-35-foot plots in Felts Park, a 28-acre fairground in the heart of Galax, transforming it into a massive, muddy parking lot sprinkled with portable toilets and a cluster of food trucks, offering loaded potatoes, funnel cake, and blooming onions. The Old Fiddler’s Convention—or just Galax, as the diehards call it—is the oldest and largest fiddlers’ convention in the world, a place where banjo pickers, guitar players, and other maestros of old-time strings gather to see how they stack up. Founded in 1935, Galax has been canceled only twice since then: once during World War II, due to travel restrictions, and again in 2020, due to the COVID-19 pandemic. Many Galaxers have been going every year for decades, some for as long as they’ve been alive. There are scads of rules: If you play clawhammer-style banjo in the bluegrass band competition, you will get disqualified. Same if you play bluegrass-style banjo in the old-time competition. If you break a string while performing, you must stop immediately. Playing time during competition is limited to two minutes and 30 seconds. And, crucially, no electric instruments of any kind are allowed, either on stage or in the tightly packed fairgrounds, where self-described hillbillies, accountants, firefighters, and session musicians pick day and night in blistering, impromptu jams amid the RVs. * Reason is more than a tool
  Aeon | 01 Oct 10:00 UTC
  If intelligence is merely optimisation then machines will outrun us. Kant tells us why human reason is so much more - by Sasha Mudd Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Illustration by Aeon/Daniel Ceng/Anadolu/Getty Images A few months ago, I sat in a packed university auditorium listening to a celebrated computer scientist explain the brilliance of large language models (LLMs). These systems optimise, he said. And they improve at optimisation on the basis of past experience. He paused. That, he said with a smile, is intelligence. The audience nodded along approvingly. Meanwhile, a philosophical alarm bell began sounding in my head. Not because the speaker’s claim was implausible. On the contrary, as a description of much contemporary artificial intelligence, it seemed apt. AI systems are built around objectives, losses, rewards, predictions, preferences, benchmarks and feedback loops. They are trained to perform better relative to some standard of success. In one form or another, they optimise. That is a great part of their technical power. What troubled me was the ease with which a definition appropriate to machines sounded like a definition of intelligence as such. That slide from engineering model to human anthropology is easy to miss. We begin by saying: this is how machines work. Then, almost without noticing, we say: this is what intelligence is. And then, more dangerously still: perhaps this is what we are. An old argument about human beings had entered the room without announcing itself. It sounded, instead, like a definition. The AI revolution has brought one of the Enlightenment’s deepest quarrels back to life – and made one side of it seem like plain common sense. In my view, the wrong side. Hence the alarm bell. I n the 18th century, philosophers were concerned not only with the question of what reason is, but with what it is good for. Is reason essentially a servant, a clever instrument for achieving ends supplied from elsewhere? Or is it sovereign, capable of judging, revising and even legislating ends for itself? Can it ultimately shape the direction of human life, or is it merely along for the ride? Everything – from science to social progress to the viability of the French Revolution – seemed to hang in the balance. David Hume , the cool-eyed Scottish anatomist of human nature, gave the most famous answer on one side: ‘Reason is, and ought only to be the slave of the passions,’ he wrote in A Treatise of Human Nature (1739). It can ‘never pretend to any other office than to serve and obey them.’ Reason does not command. It defers. It does not tell us what to want or what might be worth wanting. Instead, i
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-real-intelligence-is-something-more-than-optimisation

The strange intimacy in the way the surface gives way and something underneath begins to show makes it difficult to turn away, not because it is unfamiliar, but because it isn’t.   FULL TEXT: One day, toward the end of April, Sandan Rabdanov was driving an 18-wheeler through Wyoming when he received a text from a friend. “There was a big raid in Wyoming yesterday. Be careful,” the message read. He needed to skip his rest stop in Rock Springs and leave the state, or risk running into Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE). Rabdanov, 32, is a Russian citizen of the Buryat minority, a Mongol ethnic group living mostly around Lake Baikal in southern Siberia. The message said that two other Buryat truck drivers nearby — one of them a Buddhist lama — had just been arrested by ICE. So Rabdanov kept driving through the night until he cleared the state line. “I felt I was almost busted,” Rabdanov told New Lines , in one of several interviews conducted over the past few months. Now living in Brooklyn, Rabdanov fled Russia in 2022 for fear of persecution for his activism against the Ukraine war, though that was only the latest among many episodes of feeling targeted and discriminated against. One thread of the fraught tensions that exist between Russians and Buryats dates back to the 17th century, when Buryat nomadic tribes faced the arrival of Russian Cossacks colonizing the lands around Lake Baikal. In 1727, the Russian Empire and the Qing dynasty established the Sino-Russian border and cut off Buryats from the rest of the Mongol people, who today live mostly in Mongolia and Southern Mongolia (officially known as Inner Mongolia, in China). The Russian conquest was violent, with documented abuses and forced conversions to Christianity. It was in this borderland, in the village of Tsagan-Chelutai — 130 miles from the junction between Russia, Mongolia and China — that Rabdanov was born in 1994. His father had run a collective farm during the Soviet period and later worked in the district administration. His mother taught English. At home, everyone spoke Buryat. “When I was a toddler, I didn’t know any Russian,” said Rabdanov. That changed at kindergarten, where Russian was the only language allowed. The district was two-thirds Buryat, but it offered no Buryat classes until ninth grade. In elementary school, walking in the city of Chita with his sister, he tossed away a banana peel, and two Russian elderly ladies called him a monkey. “I didn’t realize what they meant.   https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/ice-turns-its-sights-on-buryat-asylum-seekers/

* AI companies want to embed safety evaluators, but countries need their own
  Rest of World | 30 Sep 10:00 UTC
  At a Rest of World event in New York last week, speakers explored how countries sidelined by the U.S.-China race can retain some control over safety standards as they adopt models from OpenAI, Anthropic, and others.
  FULL TEXT: The growing number of instances of artificial intelligence agents hacking the websites of governments and other institutions is a warning to countries using American models to do their own safety evaluations, experts said at a Rest of World event in New York last week. An OpenAI agent hacked into an Australian national healthcare database and accessed “public and non-public files,” Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said last week. It was the first such reported case. Days later, OpenAI said it had alerted “dozens” of global institutions that its AI agents had acted improperly to get information from their websites, sometimes circumventing security measures. The concentration of power is itself a safety risk.” Amba Kak, co-executive director, AI Now Institute The disclosures came on the heels of other incidents reported by OpenAI, Anthropic, and Meta in recent months, prompting Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei to call for an industrywide slowdown in AI development. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and xAI CEO Elon Musk backed his call, even as President Donald Trump rejected it. Leaving AI safety in the hands of a few companies is a threat to the sovereignty of nations, in particular smaller countries that lack the resources to assess the models or demand greater accountability, Amba Kak, co-executive director at AI Now Institute, an independent research organization, said at the Rest of World event. The Australia hack is “another example of the most shoddy, irresponsible cybersecurity hygiene on the part of some of the most powerful, wealthy source companies in the world,” she said. “The concentration of power is itself a safety risk.” OpenAI said the Australia incident occurred in June, and that the company became aware of it in August, and informed the Australian government in September via an email to a generic inbox. It took OpenAI “way too long to inform the government what had occurred, and the nature of the way that that notification occurred as well was unacceptable,” Albanese told reporters. OpenAI was not “as fast as we would have liked, but we are trying to balance our desire for transparency with gaining a clear understanding from petabytes of agent activity logs, and working with impacted organizations,” Altman wrote on X.
